, with
P(\Lambda)
being neither uniformly distributed nor a deterministic distribution,
W=\Lambda
and
Y=X\oplus\Lambda
. Here we have
X\vDash Y
since
\Lambda
is non-uniform as well as
X\vDash Y|W
since
\Lambda
is non-deterministic. Therefore
X\vDash Y|W
does not satisfy the Clus
4
property. However since
Y\not\mathrel{\perp\mkern-11.0mu\perp}W|X
(and
X
is exogenous to the same holds under interventions on
X
),
X\vDash Y|W
is not reducible in the fourth argument and is an Irred
4
affects relation.

Theorem

4.12
in the main text proves that clustering in the first, second and third argument of an unconditional affects relation
X\vDash Y|\mathrm{do}(Z)
implies fine-tuning of the underlying causal model. Here we extend the result to conditional affects relations and clustering in the fourth argument, introduced in this section. Due to the restriction to the subset
X(W)
of
X
in
Lemma

E.6
, which is only relevant when considering non-trivial conditional affects relations with
W\neq\emptyset
, our result for the conditional case is not fully general and applies to cases where
X(W)=X
, or equivalently where
X
is not a cause of
W
.
24
24

24

X
is a cause of
W
is equivalent to saying that there exists an element
e_{X}
that has a directed path to some element
e_{W}
of
W
, therefore
X
is not a cause of
W
is equivalent to
X\cap\mathrm{anc}(W)=\emptyset
, which is equivalent to
X(W)=X
for
X(W)
as defined in
Lemma

E.6
.
We conjecture that the theorem also holds more generally, without this restriction and leave a proof of this to future work, as it is not directly relevant for the main results of this paper.

Theorem E.20

[Clustering implies fine-tuning (conditional version)]

Any affects relation
X\vDash Y|\mathrm{do}(Z),W
that satisfies Clus
i
for
i\in\{1,2,3,4\}
and generated in a causal graph
\mathcal{G}
where
X
is not a cause of
W
, necessarily arises from a fine-tuned causal model on
\mathcal{G}
.

Proof:

The proofs for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
are entirely analogous as those of
Theorem

4.12
whenever we are given that
X
is not a cause of
W
. This is because the main step of the proof is to infer from
X\vDash Y\given\mathrm{do}(Z)
that
(X\not\perp^{d}Y|Z)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(XZ)}}
, and the analogous inference holds for conditional affects relations when
X
is not a cause of
W
, due to
Lemma

E.6
i.e.,
X\vDash Y\given\mathrm{do}(Z),W
implies
(X\not\perp^{d}Y|ZW)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(XZ)}}
whenever
X
is not a cause of
W
. We therefore do not repeat the proof.

The proof for
i=4
is also analogous, but we repeat it for completeness. Suppose that
X\vDash Y\given\mathrm{do}(Z),W
holds and is a Clus
4
affects relation. This implies in particular that for all
e_{W}\in W
,
X\not\vDash Y|\mathrm{do}(Z),e_{W}
. Writing this out, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(XZ)}}(Y|XZW)&\neq P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(Z)}}(Y|ZW)\\
P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(XZ)}}(Y|XZe_{W})&=P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(Z)}}(Y|Ze_{W}),\qquad\forall e_{W}\in W.\end{split}

(55)

Further, using
Lemma

E.5
, the non-affects relations
X\not\vDash Y|\mathrm{do}(Z),e_{W}
imply the conditional independences
(X\mathrel{\perp\mkern-11.0mu\perp}Y|Z,e_{W})_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(XZ)}}
. Now consider the corresponding d-separation for each
e_{W}\in W
, we can either have
(X\perp^{d}Y|Z,e_{W})_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(XZ)}}
or
(X\not\perp^{d}Y|Z,e_{W})_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(XZ)}}
. In the latter case we could have a d-connection with a corresponding conditional independence, which would make the model fine-tuned. So we consider the case where

(X\perp^{d}Y|Z,e_{W})_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(XZ)}}
holds for all
e_{W}\in W
(for all other cases the above argument establishes the result about fine-tuning). However, by assumption that
X
is not a cause of
W
, we have
X(W)=W
and we know from
Lemma

E.6
that
(X\perp^{d}Y|Z,e_{W})_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(XZ)}}
implies
X\not\vDash Y|\{\mathrm{do}(Z),W\}
, which contradicts our initial assumption and established the result.

\square

Finally, we provide an additional result relating Clus
2
and Clus
4
(clustering in observational arguments) which is analogous to
Lemma

D.3
which links Clus
1
and Clus
3
(clustering in the interventional arguments).
However, due to the minimum cardinality for affects relations to be Clus
4
, it only holds in a more restricted case.

Corollary E.21

For any unconditional affects relation
X\vDash Y|\mathrm{do}(Z)
which is Clus
2
and has
\left|Y\right|\geq 3
,

X\vDash s_{Y}|\mathrm{do}(Z),\tilde{s}_{Y}
is Clus
4
for each partition
s_{Y}\tilde{s}_{Y}=Y
with
\left|\tilde{s}_{Y}\right|\geq 2
.

Proof:

Any
X\vDash Y|\mathrm{do}(Z)
which is Clus
2
, by
Lemma

E.2
.3 is equivalent to
X\vDash s_{Y}|\mathrm{do}(Z),\tilde{s}_{Y}
for each partition
s_{Y}\tilde{s}_{Y}=Y
.

If for the case of
\tilde{s}_{Y}\geq 2
– which is only possible for
Y\geq 2
– this affects relation were not Clus
4
, there would exist
X\affects s_{Y}|\mathrm{do}(Z),\tilde{s}^{\prime}_{Y}
with
\tilde{s}^{\prime}_{Y}\subsetneq\tilde{s}_{Y}
, implying
X\affects s_{Y}\tilde{s}^{\prime}_{Y}|\mathrm{do}(Z)
by
Lemma

E.2
.1. This is in contradiction to the original affects relation being Clus
2
and proves the claim.

\square

The above result is only established for unconditional affects relations, and the full generalization of these statements to conditional relations remains open.

E.3
Compatibility for conditional affects relations

We provide the generalization of
Definition

6.1
to conditional affects relations, as given in
[
59
]
, yet simplified with regard to the embedding.

Corollary E.22

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a set of ORVs from a set of RVs
S
and a poset
\mathcal{T}
with an embedding
\mathcal{E}
. Then a set of affects relations
\mathscr{A}
is said to be
compatible
with
\mathcal{E}
(or satisfies
compat
) if the following condition holds:

•

Let
X,Y\subset S
be disjoint non-empty sets of RVs,
Z,W\subset S
further disjoint sets of RVs, potentially empty.
If (
X\affects Y\given\doo(Z),W
)
\in\mathscr{A}
and is Irred
1
, then

\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{W}\mathcal{Z})\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{X})\,.

This generalization follows from the unconditional definition of compatibility by applying
Lemma

E.2
.

Appendix F
Relaxing compatibility to atomic affects relations

As we have seen, the causal structure is a graph whose nodes are associated with individual random variables or physical systems (of some theory) while the signalling structure, which is captured by affects relations in our framework, is defined on sets of random variables. In other frameworks, which consider notions of signalling defined for quantum systems in a circuit, it has been shown that in unitary quantum circuits, signalling relations between individual in/output systems are sufficient to fully characterize all the signalling relations.
25
25

25

This is not the case in non-unitary circuits. There, we can have signalling between sets of in/output systems without signalling between individual elements of the set (analogous to clustered affects relations we have encountered in
Section

4.2
).
This property of unitary circuits is referred to as atomicity
[
44
]
. In d-separation causal models, whenever there is an inequivalence between causation and signalling, this indicates fine-tuning
[
59
]
. The assumption of a unitary quantum circuit is closely related to the assumption of a faithful (not fine-tuned) causal model in the quantum case, as discussed in
[
3
]
.

This motivates us to consider a similar property for affects relations (which capture signalling), and call an affects relation atomic if it originates from a single RV:
X\affects Y\given\doo(Z)
with
\left|X\right|=1
.
26
26

26

In contrast to the atomicity property on unitaries, this notion still allows the second argument of the affects relation to refer to sets of RVs. As this is not relevant for our main results, we leave an exploration of atomicity in different arguments to future work, focusing only on the first argument here.

This is particularly appealing as all such affects relations are Irred
1
by construction.

Then we ask, when does imposing compatibility only for atomic affects relations with a given space-time imply general compatibility for all irreducible affects relations. We find that this is not true in general but it is true when we either restrict to faithful causal models or to conical space-times, revealing yet another correspondence between these distinct causality concepts.

Definition F.1
(compat-atomic)

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a set of ORVs from a set of RVs
S
and a poset
\mathcal{T}
with an embedding
\mathcal{E}
. Then a set of unconditional affects relations
\mathscr{A}
satisfies
compat-atomic
with respect to
\mathcal{E}
, if it satisfies
compat
restricted to such affects relations whose first argument
X\subset S
satisfies
\left|X\right|=1
.

While
compat
implies
compat-atomic
, both notions are not equivalent for general causal models and space-times as illustrated by the following example.

\mathcal{Z}
\mathcal{X}
\mathcal{Y}
a

Figure 11
:

Combined representation of the causal structure of
Example

18
and its space-time embedding into 1+1-Minkowski space-time satisfying
compat-atomic
and accordingly,
\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Z})=\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{X})
. Even though
XZ\affects Y
, we have
\bar{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{Y})\not\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Z})
and hence violate
compat
.

(

\not\Rightarrow

compat
)

, with a model satisfying
Y=X\oplus Z
. Then for any distribution on the parentless nodes
X
and
Z
, the affects relations with a singleton in their first argument (
X\affects Y\given\doo(Z)
and
Z\affects Y\given\doo(X)
) imply
\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Z})\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{X})
as well as
\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{X})\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{Z})
as compatibility conditions.
Therefore
compat-atomic
yields
\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Z})=\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{X})
.
For 1+1-Minkowski space-time, this is satisfied in an embedding where
\mathcal{Z}\prec\mathcal{X}\prec a\succ\mathcal{Y}
for
a\in\mathcal{T}
.
By contrast, due to
XZ\affects Y
being Irred
1
,
compat
additionally demands
\bar{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{Y})\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Z})
, which is not satisfied in this embedding. Note however that the stronger condition imposed by
compat
is necessary for having no superluminal signalling: In the given embedding, an agent with access to
Y
can learn about the parity of
X
and
Z
outside the future light cone of any of these variables.

We now show that analogous to the main result of
Theorem

6.3
/
Theorem

G.10
, under almost identical restrictions on either the space-time or the causal model / affects relations, the desired implication does hold, hinting at a deeper correspondence between conical space-times and causal models without clustered affects relations.
For this, recall that according to
Definition

4.2
, an affects relation
X\affects Y\given\doo(Z)
to be Irred
1
is actually just a short-hand for a whole family of affects relations to hold:

s_{X}\affects Y\given\doo(Z\tilde{s}_{X})\quad\forall s_{X}\subseteq X\,,

(56)

where
\tilde{s}_{X}:=X\setminus s_{X}
.
Therefore, we have a rather analog situation to
Lemma

G.9
concerning irreducibility in the third argument, and can follow the same idea for the proof.

Lemma F.2

Let
\mathscr{A}
be a set of unconditional affects relations.
Then for any non-degenerate conical embedding
\mathcal{E}
(cf.
Definition

G.1
) into space-time
\mathcal{T}
,

\hyperref@@ii[def:compat-atomic]{\textbf{compat-atomic}}\implies\hyperref@@ii[def:compat]{\textbf{compat}}.

(57)

Proof:

By definition,
X\affects Y\given\doo(Z)
being Irred
1
implies

e_{X}\affects Y\given\doo(ZX\setminus e_{X})
.

compat-atomic
then yields

\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Z})\cap\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{X}\setminus e_{\mathcal{X}})\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}(e_{\mathcal{X}})\quad\forall e_{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathcal{X}\,.

(58)

With
Lemma

G.7
for
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Z}
and
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{X}
it follows that

\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Z}\mathcal{X})=\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Z}\mathcal{X}\setminus e_{\mathcal{X}})\quad\forall e_{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathcal{X}\,.

(59)

We proceed analogous to
Lemma

G.9
, deriving that the embedding is either degenerate or

\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Z}\mathcal{Y})\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{X})\,.

(60)

This is precisely
compat
, concluding the proof.

\square

Lemma F.3

Let
\mathscr{A}
be a set of unconditional affects relations which does not contain any affects relations that are Clus
1
.
Then for any non-degenerate embedding
\mathcal{E}
of
\mathscr{A}
into
any
space-time
\mathcal{T}
,

\hyperref@@ii[def:compat-atomic]{\textbf{compat-atomic}}\implies\hyperref@@ii[def:compat]{\textbf{compat}}.

(61)

In particular, this holds for any faithful causal model.

Proof:

By definition,
X\affects Y\given\doo(Z)
being Irred
1
implies

e_{X}\affects Y\given\doo(ZX\setminus e_{X})
.

By
Lemma

D.4
, the absence of Clus
1
relations in
\mathscr{A}
implies that there exists
e^{1}_{X}\in X
such that
e^{1}_{X}\affects Y\given\doo(Z)
, and therefore, by
compat-atomic
,
\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Z})\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}(e^{1}_{\mathcal{X}})
, which again implies
\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Z}e^{1}_{\mathcal{X}})=\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Z})
.
However, due to Irred
1
, we also have
X\setminus e^{1}_{X}\affects Y\given\doo(Ze^{1}_{X})
.
Invoking again the absence of Clus
1
relations and applying
Lemma

D.4
, we know that there exists
e^{2}_{X}\in X
such that
e^{2}_{X}\affects Y\given\doo(Ze^{1}_{X})
, yielding
\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Z}e^{1}_{\mathcal{X}})\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}(e^{2}_{\mathcal{X}})
.
Together by the earlier condition on
\bar{\mathcal{F}}(e^{1}_{\mathcal{X}})
, this yields
\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Z})\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}(e^{2}_{\mathcal{X}})
.

As Irred
1
for the original affects relation implies the same for
X\setminus e^{1}_{X}\affects Y\given\doo(Ze^{1}_{X})
, we can now repeat this procedure recursively, yielding
\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Z})\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}(e_{\mathcal{X}})\ \forall e_{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathcal{X}
. Taken together, this is equivalent to
\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Z})\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{X})
, yielding the claim.

\square

Using the absence of affects relations with Clus
2
or Clus
3
as a condition, similar results can be obtained for atomic compatibility in the second and third argument.

Analogous to
Theorem

6.3
and
Theorem

6.4
, this demonstrates another useful implication which does not hold in general space-times and causal models, but does hold when either restricting to conical space-times or restricting to causal models without a type of clustering.
A difference however is that
Theorem

6.4
uses the (stronger) absence of Clus
3
while the above result uses only the absence of Clus
1
, indeed the former was related to Irred
3
affects relations while the latter is about Irred
1
affects relations.
Moreover, while our main results imply an ability to reduce compatibility statements for higher-order affects relations to equivalent statements for 0
{}^{\text{th}}
-order relations, the results of this section enable a reduction of compatibility statements for non-atomic affects relations to equivalent statements for atomic relations. There is scope for exploring further relations and implications of these results, as well as other possible correspondences between conical space-times and causal models without clustering of a certain type. We leave this for future work.

Remark F.4

Due to
Lemma

G.8
, any affects relation
X\affects Y\given\doo(Z)
irreducible in its third argument implies that for
any
embedding satisfying
compat-atomic
, we have

\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{X})\cap\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Z}\setminus e_{\mathcal{Z}})\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}(e_{\mathcal{Z}})
.
Conjoining this with
Equation

58
(implied by
compat-atomic
), we obtain

\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y})\cap\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Z}\setminus e_{\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Z}})\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}(e_{\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Z}})\quad\forall e_{\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Z}}\in\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Z}\,.

(62)

Since
compat
implies
compat-atomic
, the same follows from imposing
compat
for an Irred
3
affects relation.
Hence, even without imposing the restrictions of
Theorem

6.3
or
Theorem

6.4
, namely the absence of clustering or conical space-times, we obtain this weaker type of interchangeability of
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Z}
directly from
compat-atomic
(or
compat
). However, this is not strong enough to yield the correspondence of
Equation

26
between causal inference statements and compatibility statements.

Appendix G
Proofs of results

G.1
Proofs for Section 4

See
4.1

Proof:

Suppose by contradiction that
X\not\vDash Y|\mathrm{do}(Z)
(equivalently
P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(XZ)}}(Y|XZ)=P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(Z)}}(Y|Z)
) and
(X\not\mathrel{\perp\mkern-11.0mu\perp}Y|Z)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}}(XZ)}
. The latter is equivalent to saying that there exist distinct values
z
of
Z
and distinct values
x
and
x^{\prime}
of
X
such that
P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(XZ)}}(Y|X=x,Z=z)\neq P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(XZ)}}(Y|X=x^{\prime},Z=z)
. However, this implies that
P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(XZ)}}(Y|XZ)
does have a non-trivial dependence on
X
and contradicts
P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(XZ)}}(Y|XZ)=P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(Z)}}(Y|Z)
(which would imply that it is equal to an
X
-independent quantity). This proves the result.

\square

See
4.11

Proof:

Clus
1

\Rightarrow
Irred
1

Suppose that
X\vDash Y|\mathrm{do}(Z)
holds and satisfies Clus
1
but not Irred
1
i.e., it is Red
1
. Then by
Definition

4.2
and
Definition

4.8
of these properties, we have the following requirements:
\tilde{s}_{X}\not\vDash Y|\mathrm{do}(Z)
for all
\tilde{s}_{X}\subsetneq X
and
\exists\tilde{s}_{X}\subsetneq X
, with
\tilde{s}_{X}\cup s_{X}=X
such that
s_{X}\not\vDash Y|\mathrm{do}(\tilde{s}_{X}Z)
. Writing these out explicitly, we have:

\displaystyle\begin{split}P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(\tilde{s}_{X}Z)}}(Y|\tilde{s}_{X}Z)&=P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(Z)}}(Y|Z),\quad\forall\tilde{s}_{X}\subsetneq X,\\
P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(XZ)}}(Y|XZ)&=P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(\tilde{s}_{X}Z)}}(Y|\tilde{s}_{X}Z),\quad\exists\tilde{s}_{X}\subsetneq X,\end{split}

(63)

These imply
P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(XZ)}}(Y|XZ)=P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(Z)}}(Y|Z)
which is equivalent to
X\not\vDash Y|\mathrm{do}(Z)
. This contradicts our initial assumption that
X\vDash Y|\mathrm{do}(Z)
holds and therefore proves that Clus
1
implies Irred
1
for any affects relation
X\vDash Y|\mathrm{do}(Z)
.

Clus
2

\Rightarrow
Irred
2

We assume that
X\vDash Y|\mathrm{do}(Z)
holds, it is Clus
2
but not Irred
2
and derive a contradiction, which will establish that Clus
2

\Rightarrow
Irred
2
for any affects relation
X\vDash Y|\mathrm{do}(Z)
.
Clus
2
implies the following

P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(XZ)}}(s_{Y}|XZ)=P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(Z)}}(s_{Y}|Z)\quad\forall s_{Y}\subsetneq Y.

(64)

On the other hand, Red
2
implies that there exists
s_{Y}\subsetneq Y
such that
X\naffects s_{Y}\given\doo(Z),\tilde{s}_{Y}
. However, by
Lemma

E.2
.3, both of these together imply
X\affects\tilde{s}_{Y}\given\doo(Z)
, which contradicts our assumption that
X\affects Y\given\doo(Z)
is Clus
2
.

Clus
3

\Rightarrow
Irred
3

As before, we assume that
X\vDash Y|\mathrm{do}(Z)
holds and satisfies Clus
3
and Red
3
. From
Definition

4.10
and
Definition

4.4
this implies the following conditions.

\displaystyle\begin{split}P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(X\tilde{s}_{Z})}}(Y|X\tilde{s}_{Z})&=P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(\tilde{s}_{Z})}}(Y|\tilde{s}_{Z}),\quad\forall\tilde{s}_{Z}\subsetneq Z,\\
P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(XZ)}}(Y|XZ)&=P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(X\tilde{s}_{Z})}}(Y|X\tilde{s}_{Z}),\quad\exists\tilde{s}_{Z}\subsetneq Z,\\
P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(Z)}}(Y|Z)&=P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(\tilde{s}_{Z})}}(Y|\tilde{s}_{Z}),\quad\exists\tilde{s}_{Z}\subsetneq Z.\end{split}

(65)

Combining these, we obtain
P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(XZ)}}(Y|XZ)=P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(Z)}}(Y|Z)
which is equivalent to
X\not\vDash Y|\mathrm{do}(Z)
, which contradicts our initial assumption and therefore proves the claim.

\square

See
4.12

Proof:

Clus
1

\Rightarrow
fine-tuning
Suppose that
X\vDash Y|\mathrm{do}(Z)
is a Clus
1
affects relation, which means that
|X|\geq 2
and
s_{X}\not\vDash Y|\mathrm{do}(Z)
for all
s_{X}\subsetneq X
.
From the proof of Lemma IV.3 of
[
59
]
, it follows that
X\vDash Y|\mathrm{do}(Z)
implies that there exists
e_{X}\in X
with a directed path from
e_{X}
to
Y
in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}}(XZ)
, which in turn implies
(e_{X}\not\perp^{d}Y|Z)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}}(XZ)}
.
Further, this implies the same d-connection in the graph where we intervene only on
e_{X}
, i.e.,
(e_{X}\not\perp^{d}Y|Z)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}}(e_{X}Z)}
.
This is because the graph
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}}(XZ)
will have the same nodes and less edges than
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}}(e_{X}Z)
(since interventions cut off incoming edges), and d-connection cannot be lost by adding edges.
However Clus
1
implies in particular that
e_{X}\not\vDash Y|\mathrm{do}(Z)
.
This in turn implies that
(e_{X}\mathrel{\perp\mkern-11.0mu\perp}Y|Z)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}}(s_{X}Z)}
(
Lemma

4.1
), which along with
(e_{X}\not\perp^{d}Y|Z)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}}(e_{X}Z)}
which we have previously established, implies that any underlying causal model giving rise to these affects relations must be fine-tuned.

Clus
2

\Rightarrow
fine-tuning
Suppose that
X\vDash Y|\mathrm{do}(Z)
is a Clus
2
affects relation, which means that
|Y|\geq 2
and
X\not\vDash s_{Y}|\mathrm{do}(Z)
for all
s_{Y}\subsetneq Y
. As before,
X\vDash Y|\mathrm{do}(Z)
implies that
(X\not\perp^{d}Y|Z)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}}(XZ)}
. By the definition of d-separation, this tells us that there must exist
e_{Y}\in Y
such that
(X\not\perp^{d}e_{Y}|Z)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}}(XZ)}
. However Clus
2
implies that for all
e_{Y}\in Y
,
X\not\vDash e_{Y}|\mathrm{do}(Z)
which gives
(X\mathrel{\perp\mkern-11.0mu\perp}e_{Y}|Z)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}}(XZ)}
for all
e_{Y}\in Y
(
Lemma

4.1
). Then it is clear that there is at least one d-connection
(X\not\perp^{d}e_{Y}|Z)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}}(XZ)}
not matched by a corresponding conditional independence, thus making the model fine-tuned.

Clus
3

\Rightarrow
fine-tuning
Suppose that
X\vDash Y|\mathrm{do}(Z)
is a Clus
3
affects relation, which means that
|Z|\geq 1
and
X\not\vDash Y|\mathrm{do}(s_{Z})
for all
s_{Z}\subsetneq Z
. As in the proof for the previous case, we start with the fact that
X\vDash Y|\mathrm{do}(Z)
implies
(X\not\perp^{d}Y|Z)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}}(XZ)}
. Since all nodes of the conditioning set
Z
are parentless in this d-connection, it follows that
(X\not\perp^{d}Y|s_{Z})_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}}(XZ)}
holds for subsets
s_{Z}\subsetneq Z
. This is because the only case where removing elements from the conditioning set could remove a d-connection is when those elements act as a collider (or descendent of a collider) on a sole unblocked path between
X
and
Y
, which is not possible if those elements are parentless. Now, observe that the only distinction between the graphs
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}}(Xs_{Z})
for
s_{Z}\subsetneq Z
and
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}}(XZ)
is that the former has a strictly larger set of edges (the incoming edges to nodes in
Z\backslash s_{Z}
are present in the former and not in the latter). Since d-connection cannot be lost by adding edges,
(X\not\perp^{d}Y|s_{Z})_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}}(XZ)}
implies
(X\not\perp^{d}Y|s_{Z})_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}}(Xs_{Z})}
. We then use the fact that Clus
3
implies
X\not\vDash Y|\mathrm{do}(s_{Z})
for all
s_{Z}\subsetneq Z
, and consequently that
(X\mathrel{\perp\mkern-11.0mu\perp}Y|s_{Z})_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}}(XZ)}
for all
s_{Z}\subsetneq Z
(
Lemma

4.1
), which when taken together with the d-connection
(X\not\perp^{d}Y|s_{Z})_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}}(Xs_{Z})}
indicates that the causal model must be fine-tuned.

\square

G.2
Proofs for Section 5

See
5.4

Proof:

In
d
+1-dim. Minkowski space-time
\mathcal{T}
, the boundary of the light cone of a space-time point
(\vec{x}_{0},t_{0})\in\mathcal{T}
is given as follows, where
\vec{x}_{0}=(x^{1}_{0},x^{2}_{0},...,x^{d}_{0})
are the
d
-dimensional spatial co-ordinates of the point (in some chosen co-ordinate system).

c^{2}(t-t_{0})^{2}=\sum_{1<i\leq d}(x^{i}-x^{i}_{0})^{2}\,.

(66)

Observe that for each time slice (fixed value of
t
), the above equation corresponds to a
d
-dimensional sphere with radius
c^{2}(t-t_{0})^{2}
. Whenever
d\geq 2
, for a fixed
t
, given any finite portion of this spherical boundary, we can determine the radius of the sphere and therefore its center, which is associated with the spatial co-ordinate
\vec{x}_{0}
. If we are considering the future light cone, this is non-empty only for
t>t_{0}
. Given a non-empty time slice of the future light cone of a space-time point
(\vec{x}_{0},t_{0})\in\mathcal{T}
, we can consider light rays emanating from each point on the boundary of the sphere and extending towards the negative
t
direction, these light rays would necessarily intersect at a unique time
t_{0}
which allows us to fully determine the original point
(\vec{x}_{0},t_{0})
, starting from any portion of the future light cone boundary on a given non-empty time slice.

Now let
L
be a finite subset of points in
d
+1-dim. Minkowski space-time and consider the joint future
f(L)=\bigcap_{x\in L}\bar{J}^{+}(x)
of all the points in
L
. The boundary of this joint future is compiled from pieces of the light cone boundaries of points in
L
. In particular, the boundary of
f(L)
associated with a non-empty time slice is compiled from spherical pieces which are portions of light cone boundaries of points in
L
, associated with that time slice (for
d=2
, these are circular arcs). We can show that each
x\in\spann(L)
contributes a unique, finite portion to the boundary of the joint future region
f(L)
at every non-empty time slice.
For this, recall that by definition, if
x\in\spann(L)
, then
x\in L^{\prime}\subseteq L
such that
f(L^{\prime})=f(L)
and there is no
L^{\prime\prime}\subsetneq L^{\prime}
satisfying
f(L^{\prime\prime})=f(L)
. If we assume that
x
doesn’t contribute a finite piece to the boundary of
f(L)
, then this yields a contradiction as it would mean that for
L^{\prime\prime}:=L^{\prime}\setminus x
, we have
f(L^{\prime\prime})=f(L)
. To show uniqueness, suppose
x\in\spann(L)
contributes a finite portion to the boundary of
f(L)
at a time slice
t
. No other
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}
can contribute the same portion of the boundary of
f(L)
, due to the argument presented in the first paragraph of this proof: each such portion allows us to uniquely reconstruct the point
x
as it is also a portion of the future light cone of
x
.

Thus, the distinct spherical portions that comprise the boundary of
f(L)
can each be mapped back uniquely to a distinct spanning element of
L
. Since
L
is arbitrary here, this implies that
f(L)
is invertible on all sets
L
with
L=\spann(L)
, which proves the conicality of Minkowski space-times with 2 or more spatial dimensions.

\square

See
5.13

We prove a generalization of this lemma from conical space-times to conical embeddings in arbitrary space-times, which are defined as follows:

Definition G.1
(Conical Embedding)

An embedding of a set
\mathcal{S}
of ORVs in a space-time
\mathcal{T}
is called
conical
if for any two finite subsets
L_{i},L_{j}\subseteq O(\mathcal{S})=\{O(X)|X\in S\}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
,

f(L_{i})=f(L_{j})\quad\implies\quad\spann(L_{i})=\spann(L_{j})

(67)

holds.

Intuitively, with this definition, we consider the condition of conicality only for the futures of all points
O(\mathcal{S})
in the image of the embedding and the intersections of their futures.
Hence, any embedding into a conical poset
\mathcal{T}
is trivially conical. However, the conicality of
\mathcal{T}
is not necessary to obtain a conical embedding:
Actually, the vast majority of embeddings into 1+1-dim. Minkowski space-time is conical as well, as for instance shown in
Table

1
.
27
27

27

Specifically, such embeddings can be non-conical only if one ORV is embedded into the future light cone surface of another,
constituting a kind of fine-tuning of the embedding.
For instance, consider the example shown in
Figure

8
, with
\mathcal{Y}
embedded on the future light cone surface of
\mathcal{Z}_{1}
and
\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Z}_{2})=\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Z}_{1}\mathcal{Z}_{2})
.
As a possible sufficient criterion for being located
“on the light cone surface”
we suggest, in purely order-theoretical terms:
For
a,b\in\mathcal{T}
,
b
is embedded in the future light cone surface of
a
iff
M=\bar{J}^{+}(a)\cap\bar{J}^{-}(b)=\{x\in\mathcal{T}|a\preceq x\preceq b\}
is totally ordered by
\prec
, i.e.
\not\exists\,b,c\in M:\ b\not\preceq\not\succeq c
.
Here,
M
is a degenerate example of a causal diamond
[
31
,
65
]
.
For Minkowski space-time, this criterion is also necessary, while singularities or self-intersecting light cones could lead to violations.

We finally state the generalization of
Lemma

5.13
:

Lemma G.2

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a set of ORVs from a poset
\mathcal{T}
with a
conical
embedding
\mathcal{E}
.
Then, for any
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathcal{S}
, the knowledge of
\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{X})
implies the locations
O(\mathcal{X}_{i})
for all its spanning elements
\mathcal{X}_{i}\in\spann(\mathcal{X})
.

Proof:

Recalling that
O(\mathcal{X})=\{O(\mathcal{X}_{i})\mid\mathcal{X}_{i}\in\mathcal{X}\}\subset O(\mathcal{S})
and
f(L):=\bigcap_{x\in L}\bar{J}^{+}(x)
, then by definition, for any
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathcal{S}
,

\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{X})\equiv\bigcap_{\mathcal{X}_{i}\in\mathcal{X}}\bar{J}^{+}(O(\mathcal{X}_{i}))=f(O(\mathcal{X}))=f(O({\spann(\mathcal{X})}))\,.

(68)

As the embedding is conical, we have that for
O(\mathcal{S})\subseteq\mathcal{T}
, the conicality condition is satisfied and therefore, by
Definition

5.3
,
f(\spann(O(\mathcal{X})))
is injective (at least when restricted to
O(\mathcal{X})
).
Hence,
it remains to show that
\spann(O(\mathcal{X}))\overset{!}{=}O(\spann(\mathcal{X}))
.
To this purpose, we differentiate between the case that the embedding is degenerate or not.
If the embedding is non-degenerate, we have a bijection between
\mathcal{X}
and
L_{\mathcal{X}}
, which immediately yields
\spann(O(\mathcal{X}))=O(\spann(\mathcal{X}))
.
Otherwise, there are multiple ORVs
u_{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathcal{X}
sharing the same location.
In this case, it is easy to see that either all of them or none of them belong to
\spann(\mathcal{X})
.
28
28

28

If at least one
u_{\mathcal{X}}\ni e_{\mathcal{X}}\in\spann(\mathcal{X})
, for each allowed choice of
s_{\mathcal{X}}
(as according to the definition),
\left|s_{\mathcal{X}}\cap u_{\mathcal{X}}\right|=1
. However, due to considering the union of such sets we then regain the entirety of
u_{\mathcal{X}}\subset\bigcup s_{\mathcal{X}}
.

Therefore, we can group every set of ORVs embedded degenerately at the same location, into a single ORV embedded at the same location without changing the span. Thus we have reduced a degenerate problem to a non-degenerate one such that
\spann(O(\mathcal{X}))=O(\spann(\mathcal{X}))
holds in the former if and only if it holds in the latter. Since we have established that this always holds in the non-degenerate case, this proves the claim.

\square

As outlined in the main text, our primary results rely on showing that conicality of a poset is equivalent to another poset property called location symmetry. We proceed by motivating this property. Suppose we have a family
\{S_{i}\}_{i\in I}
where each element is a set of locations
S_{i}\subset\mathcal{T}
all of which share the same span, let us denote this by
K
. Then we can write
S_{i}=KL_{i}
(where
KL_{i}
is short for the union of the sets), making clear that the locations in
K
must be shared among the sets and here the different
L_{i}
may also share locations.
Plugging
S_{i}
for
L_{i}
and
K
for
L_{j}
into
Equation

17
, which holds in conical space-times, we obtain that the the joint futures of these sets must be identical in such space-times,

f(K)=f(S_{i})\subseteq f(S_{i}\setminus K)=f(L_{i})\,,\forall i\in I

(69)

where
f(L):=\bigcap_{x\in L}\bar{J}^{+}(x)
.
Here, the subset relation follows from the fact that removing sets from an intersection always yields a (potentially improper) superset of the original intersection. As this holds for all
i\in I
, we can replace
f(L_{i})
with
\bigcap_{i\in I}f(L_{i})
.
This yields that in conical space-times where
\spann(KL_{i})=K
for all
i\in I
(or equivalently
\bigcap_{i\in I}\spann(KL_{i})=K
),

f(KL_{i})=f(KL_{k})\quad\forall i,k\in I\quad\implies\quad f(K)\subseteq\bigcap_{i\in I}f(L_{i})

(70)

More generally, we can consider sets
S_{i}=KL_{i}
where
K
is not the common span of the sets. Then
\bigcap_{i\in I}\spann(KL_{i})\neq K
. This implies that there must be further shared locations in
\spann(KL_{i})\setminus K
for each
i
, that are commonly shared between all the
L_{i}
’s, and using conicality, it can be shown that for all
i
,
\spann(KL_{i})\setminus K
must be identical in this case (as we do in the proofs below). Based on this motivation, we have the following definition of location symmetry which captures the two alternative conditions discussed above.

Definition G.3
(Location Symmetry)

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a poset and
I
be an index set. Let
K,L_{i}\subset\mathcal{T}
discrete with
i\in I
.
Then
\mathcal{T}
satisfies
location symmetry
if

\begin{split}&f(K)\cap f(L_{i})=f(K)\cap f(L_{k})\quad\forall i,k\in I\\
\ \implies\ &f(K)\subseteq\bigcap_{i\in I}f(L_{i})\quad\text{or}\quad\exists!M\subset\mathcal{T}:\ \spann(KL_{i})\setminus K=M\neq\emptyset\quad\forall i\in I\end{split}

(71)

where

f(L):=\bigcap_{x\in L}\bar{J}^{+}(x)

If
K
or
L_{i}
is empty, we understand their intersection as being equal to the full poset
\mathcal{T}
.

Alternatively, we can also phrase location symmetry in terms of a set of ORVs
\mathcal{S}
on this poset.

Definition G.4
(Location Symmetry for ORVs)

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a poset and
I
be an index set.
Let
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}_{i}\subset\mathcal{S}
with
i\in I
, not necessarily disjoint or non-empty. Then
\mathcal{T}
satisfies
location symmetry

\begin{split}&\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Y}_{i})=\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Y}_{k})\quad\forall i,k\in I\\
\ \implies\ &\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{X})\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}\left(\textstyle{\bigcup_{i\in I}}\mathcal{Y}_{i}\right)\quad\mathrm{or}\quad\exists!M\subset\mathcal{T}:\ O(\spann(\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Y}_{k}))\setminus O(\mathcal{X})=M\neq\emptyset\quad\forall k\in I\end{split}

(72)

Especially in the respective second alternative, these definitions take care to play well with the respective sets / their locations potentially not being disjoint, as this will be of importance for considering degenerate embeddings of ORVs.

Lemma G.5

\mathcal{T}
is a conical poset if and only if it satisfies location symmetry (for points in the poset).
Further,
\mathcal{E}
is a conical embedding if and only if it satisfies location symmetry (for all its ORVs).

Proof:

For this proof, we will refer to ORVs directly, as that is more compact and matches the notation we will use for the proofs going forward.
Also, the proof for both statements is identical.
All notation matches
Definition

G.4
.

“
\Rightarrow
”
: Here we assume location symmetry and prove conicality. We only require the former for the special case
where
\mathcal{X}=\emptyset
while
\mathcal{Y}_{1}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{2}
are any disjoint sets of ORVs with
\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y}_{1})=\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y}_{2})
. Then, applying
Definition

5.10
to empty sets of ORVs, we recover
\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{X})=\mathcal{T}
. Moreover, the pre-condition
\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Y}_{1})=\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Y}_{2})
for applying location symmetry is satisfied as it is equivalent to
\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y}_{1})=\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y}_{2})
. Therefore, location symmetry implies that

\mathcal{T}=\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y}_{1}\mathcal{Y}_{2})\quad\text{or}\quad O(\spann(\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Y}_{1}))\setminus O(\mathcal{X})=O(\spann(\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Y}_{2}))\setminus O(\mathcal{X})\,.

(73)

Here, the second alternative immediately reduces to
O(\spann(\mathcal{Y}_{1}))=O(\spann(\mathcal{Y}_{2}))
, due to
\mathcal{X}
being empty.
The first is an equality as
\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y}_{1}\mathcal{Y}_{2})\subseteq\mathcal{T}
trivially holds.
Moreover, notice that the first case
\mathcal{T}=\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y}_{1}\mathcal{Y}_{2})
is equivalent to
\mathcal{T}=\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y}_{1})=\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y}_{2})
, and this can only be satisfied if both sets of ORVs are embedded into the minimal element of the poset (which may not exist). If the minimal element exists, we therefore have
O(\mathcal{Y}_{1})=O(\mathcal{Y}_{2})
, which in particular implies
O(\spann(\mathcal{Y}_{1}))=O(\spann(\mathcal{Y}_{2}))

In other words, for both alternatives of location symmetry we get that for any disjoint sets of ORVs
\mathcal{Y}_{1}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{2}
,
\bar{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{Y}_{1})=\bar{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{Y}_{2})\implies O(\spann(\mathcal{Y}_{1}))=O(\spann(\mathcal{Y}_{2}))
, reproducing the definition of conicality (
Equation

17
).

“
\Leftarrow
”
: Let
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}_{i}\subset\mathcal{S}
with
i\in I
such that

\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Y}_{i})=\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Y}_{k})\quad\forall i,k\in I\,.

(74)

We now show that in any conical space-time, this implies one of the two conditions in the second line of
Equation

72
, thus proving location symmetry.

Applying the property
Equation

17
of conical space-times to
Equation

74
, we see that for any choice of
i\in I
, we can deduce the value of
O(\mathcal{A})
for all
\mathcal{A}\in\spann(\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Y}_{i})
for ORVs embedded in any conical poset.
This implies for the set of respective locations to be identical.

O(\spann(\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Y}_{i}))=O(\spann(\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Y}_{k}))\quad\forall i,k\in I\,.

(75)

We now consider two cases and show that these yield respectively the two alternatives for location symmetry, thus concluding the proof.

1.

Case 1 (
\exists i:O(\mathcal{X})=O(\spann(\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Y}_{i}))
) :
In this case, we can deduce that the same holds for all
\mathcal{Y}_{k}
with
k\in I
, due to
Equation

74
. Hence,

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{X})

\displaystyle=\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Y}_{i})

(76)

\displaystyle\implies\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{X})

\displaystyle\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y}_{i})\quad\forall i\in I

(77)

\displaystyle\iff\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{X})

\displaystyle\subseteq\bigcap_{i\in I}\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y}_{i})=\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}\left(\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathcal{Y}_{i}\right)\,,

(78)

yielding the first alternative of
Equation

72
.

2.

Case 2 (
\forall i:O(\mathcal{X})\neq O(\spann(\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Y}_{i}))
) :

This implies that
O(\spann(\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Y}_{i}))\setminus O(\mathcal{X})\neq\emptyset
.
We have already established in
Equation

75
that in conical space-times where
Equation

74
is satisfied,
O(\spann(\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Y}_{k}))
must be identical for all
i\in I
, this implies the same holds for
O(\spann(\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Y}_{i}))\setminus O(\mathcal{X})
, and yields the second alternative of
Equation

72
.

\square

We conclude by providing a weaker, but significantly shorter version of the second alternative that will be useful for clarity in some later proofs.

Lemma G.6

Let
M\subseteq\mathcal{T}
non-empty. Let
I,\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}_{k}
as in
Definition

G.4
. Then

O(\spann(\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Y}_{k}))\setminus O(\spann(\mathcal{X}))=M\quad\forall k\in I\quad\implies\quad\bigcap_{k}O(\mathcal{Y}_{k})\neq\emptyset\,.

(79)

Proof:

First, we see that
\spann(\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Y}_{i})\setminus\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathcal{Y}_{i}
.
As this carries over to their respective locations, we obtain

\displaystyle\emptyset\neq M=O(\spann(\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Y}_{k}))\setminus O(\spann(\mathcal{X}))\quad\forall k\in I

(80)

\displaystyle\iff\

\displaystyle\emptyset\neq M=O(\spann(\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Y}_{k}))\cap O(\mathcal{Y}_{k})\setminus O(\spann(\mathcal{X}))\quad\forall k\in I

(81)

\displaystyle\implies\

\displaystyle\emptyset\neq M\subseteq O(\spann(\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Y}_{k}))\cap O(\mathcal{Y}_{k})\quad\forall k\in I

(82)

\displaystyle\implies\

\displaystyle\emptyset\neq M\subseteq\bigcap_{k}O(\spann(\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Y}_{k}))\cap O(\mathcal{Y}_{k})

(83)

\displaystyle\implies\

\displaystyle\emptyset\neq\bigcap_{k}O(\mathcal{Y}_{k})\,,

(84)

where the last implication holds as intersecting larger sets always yields a (potentially improper) superset.

\square

G.3
Proofs for Section 6

To prove
Theorem

6.3
, we require some auxiliary lemmas, which we first prove before stating and proving the main theorem.

Lemma G.7

Let
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{S}
. Then

\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{A})\cap\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{B}\setminus e_{\mathcal{B}})\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}(e_{\mathcal{B}})\ \forall e_{\mathcal{B}}\in\mathcal{B}\quad\implies\quad\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B})=\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}\setminus e_{\mathcal{B}})\ \forall e_{\mathcal{B}}\in\mathcal{B}\,.

(85)

Proof:

Let
e^{i}_{\mathcal{B}}:=e_{\mathcal{B}}
. We can transform

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{A})\cap\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{B}\setminus e^{i}_{\mathcal{B}})

\displaystyle\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}(e^{i}_{\mathcal{B}})

(86)

\displaystyle\implies\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{A})\cap\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{B}\setminus e^{i}_{\mathcal{B}})

\displaystyle\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{A})\cap\bar{\mathcal{F}}(e^{i}_{\mathcal{B}})

\displaystyle\forall e^{i}_{\mathcal{B}}\in\mathcal{B}

(87)

\displaystyle\implies\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{A})\cap\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{B}\setminus e_{\mathcal{B}}^{i})

\displaystyle\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{A})\cap\bar{\mathcal{F}}(e_{\mathcal{B}}^{i})\cap\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{B}\setminus\{e^{i}_{\mathcal{B}},e^{j}_{\mathcal{B}}\})

\displaystyle\forall e_{\mathcal{B}}^{i},e_{\mathcal{B}}^{j}\in\mathcal{B}

(88)

\displaystyle\iff\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{A})\cap\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{B}\setminus e_{\mathcal{B}}^{i})

\displaystyle\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{A})\cap\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{B}\setminus e_{\mathcal{B}}^{j})

\displaystyle\forall e_{\mathcal{B}}^{i},e_{\mathcal{B}}^{j}\in\mathcal{B}\,.\!\!\!

(89)

Combining these for all
e_{\mathcal{B}}
, we get

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{A})\cap\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{B}\setminus e_{\mathcal{B}}^{i})

\displaystyle=\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{A})\cap\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{B}\setminus e_{\mathcal{B}}^{j})\quad\forall e_{\mathcal{B}}^{i},e_{\mathcal{B}}^{j}\in\mathcal{B}

(90)

\displaystyle\implies\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B})

\displaystyle=\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}\setminus e_{\mathcal{B}})\quad\forall e_{\mathcal{B}}\in\mathcal{B}\,.

(91)

This is precisely the claim.

\square

Lemma G.8

Let
\mathscr{A}
be a set of unconditional affects relations and
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathscr{A}
consist of all affects relations in
\mathscr{A}
that are Irred
3
.
Then for any non-degenerate embedding
\mathcal{E}
into an arbitrary space-time
\mathcal{T}
satisfying
compat
we have

(X\affects Y\given\doo(Z))\in\mathscr{A}^{\prime}\quad\implies\quad\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{X})\cap\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Z}\setminus e_{\mathcal{Z}})\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}(e_{\mathcal{Z}})\ \forall e_{\mathcal{Z}}\in\mathcal{Z}\,.

(92)

Proof:

As
X\affects Y\given\doo(Z)
satisfies Irred
3
, we have for
\tilde{Z}:=Z\backslash e_{Z}

\forall e_{Z}\in Z:\quad e_{Z}\affects Y\given\doo(\tilde{Z})\quad\text{or}\quad e_{Z}\affects Y\given\doo(\tilde{Z}X)\,,

(93)

either of which is Irred
1
, if it holds.
Therefore, by
compat
, we get

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y})\cap\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\tilde{\mathcal{Z}})\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}(e_{\mathcal{Z}})\quad\text{or}\quad\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y})\cap\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{X}\tilde{\mathcal{Z}})\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}(e_{\mathcal{Z}})

(94)

\displaystyle\implies\

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{X})\cap\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\tilde{\mathcal{Z}})\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}(e_{\mathcal{Z}})

(95)

for any
e_{Z}
.

\square

With these three auxiliary lemmas in place, we can continue to prove the theorem.

Lemma G.9

Let
\mathscr{A}
be a set of unconditional affects relations and
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathscr{A}
consist of all affects relations in
\mathscr{A}
that are Irred
3
.
Then for any non-degenerate conical embedding
\mathcal{E}
into a space-time
\mathcal{T}
satisfying
compat
, we have

(X\affects Y\given\doo(Z))\in\mathscr{A}^{\prime}\quad\implies\quad\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y})\cap\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{X})\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Z})\,.

(96)

Proof:

By conjoining
Lemma

G.8
with
Lemma

G.7
for
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{Z}
, for any affects relation
X\affects Y\given\doo(Z)
irreducible in the third argument, we get

\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Z})=\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Z}\setminus e_{\mathcal{Z}})\quad\forall e_{\mathcal{Z}}\in\mathcal{Z}\,.

(97)

Due to conicality of the embedding,
Lemma

G.5
implies that
\mathcal{T}
satisfies location symmetry for points in
O(\mathcal{S})
.
By using this property on the chain of equalities (setting
\mathcal{X}
in
Equation

72
to
\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Y}
, and
\mathcal{Y}_{i}
to
\mathcal{Z}_{i}=\mathcal{Z}\setminus e_{\mathcal{Z}}^{i}
) as well as the transformation of
Lemma

G.6
, we arrive at

\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Y})\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Z})\quad\text{or}\quad\bigcap_{i}O(\mathcal{Z}_{i})\neq\emptyset\,,

(98)

where
Z_{i}:=Z\setminus e_{Z}^{i}
.

For the second alternative, beware that the different
\mathcal{Z}_{i}
are not disjoint.
Nonetheless,
\bigcap_{i}\mathcal{Z}_{i}=\emptyset
.
Therefore, multiple ORVs must share the same location.
As this corresponds to a degenerate embedding, we arrive at the claim.

\square

See
6.3

We prove a generalization of this theorem from conical space-times to conical embeddings in arbitrary space-times (cf.
Definition

G.1
).

Theorem G.10

Let
\mathscr{A}
be a set of unconditional affects relations and
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathscr{A}
consist of all affects relations in
\mathscr{A}
that are both Irred
1
and Irred
3
.
Then for any non-degenerate conical embedding
\mathcal{E}
(cf.
Definition

G.1
into a space-time
\mathcal{T}
satisfying
compat
, we have

(X\affects Y\given\doo(Z))\in\mathscr{A}^{\prime}\quad\implies\quad\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y})\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{X})\cap\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Z})\,.

(99)

In particular, this holds when the space-time itself is conical.

Proof:

Due to
Lemma

G.9
,
compat
implies the following for an Irred
1
and Irred
3
affects relation

(X\affects Y\given\doo(Z))\in\mathscr{A}^{\prime}\quad\implies\quad\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y})\cap\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{X})\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Z})\,.

(100)

Conjoining this with
compat
for this affects relation (which implies
\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y})\cap\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Z})\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{X})
) and using elementary set theory yields

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y})\cap\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Z})\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{X})\cap\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y})\quad\text{and}\quad\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y})\cap\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{X})\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Z})\cap\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y})

(101)

\displaystyle\implies\

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y})\cap\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{X})=\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y})\cap\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Z})

(102)

Due to conicality,
Lemma

G.5
implies that
\mathcal{T}
satisfies location symmetry.
Using location symmetry, we arrive at

\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y})\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Z})\quad\text{or}\quad O(\mathcal{X})\cap O(\mathcal{Z})\neq\emptyset\,.

(103)

As the second option would imply the embedding to be degenerate, the claim, corresponding to the first option, follows.

\square

See
6.4

Proof:

Every affects relation
X\vDash Y|\mathrm{do}(Z)
in
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
is Irred
1
and Irred
3
.
By
Lemma

D.6
, due to the absence of affects relations with Clus
3
, there exists
e^{1}_{X}\in X
such that
e^{1}_{X}\affects Y
. Therefore, for any compatible embedding
\mathcal{E}
, we have
\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y})\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}(e^{1}_{\mathcal{X}})
.
However, due to
X\vDash Y|\mathrm{do}(Z)
being Irred
1
, we also have
X\setminus e^{1}_{X}\affects Y\given\doo(Ze^{1}_{X})
, which again by
Lemma

D.6
implies that there is
e^{2}_{X}\in X\setminus e^{1}_{X}
such that
e^{2}_{X}\affects Y
.
Repeating this argument recursively, we find that any compatible embedding
\mathcal{E}
of such affects relations in a space-time will satisfy
\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y})\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}(e_{\mathcal{X}})\ \forall e_{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathcal{X}
, or in short,
\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y})\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{X})
.

Next we use the fact that
X\vDash Y|\mathrm{do}(Z)
is Irred
3
, which gives
s_{Z}\affects Y\given\doo(\tilde{s}_{Z}X)
or
s_{Z}\affects Y\given\doo(\tilde{s}_{Z})
for all choices of
s_{Z}\subsetneq Z
.
Applying
Lemma

D.6
, both these alternatives yield
\exists e^{1}_{Z}\in Z
s.t.
e^{1}_{Z}\affects Y
and
\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y})\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}(e^{1}_{\mathcal{Z}})
.
Moreover,
X\vDash Y|\mathrm{do}(Z)
being Irred
3
implies in particular that
Z\setminus e^{1}_{Z}\affects Y\given\doo(e_{Z}^{1}X)
or
Z\setminus e^{1}_{Z}\affects Y\given\doo(e_{Z}^{1})
, both of which imply by
Lemma

D.6
that
\exists e^{2}_{Z}\in Z\setminus e^{1}_{Z}
s.t.
e^{2}_{Z}\affects Y
.
Repeating this procedure recursively, we obtain that for any compatible embedding
\mathcal{E}
of these affects relations, we have
\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y})\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}(e_{\mathcal{Z}})\ \forall e_{\mathcal{Z}}\in\mathcal{Z}
, or in short,
\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Y})\subseteq\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}(\mathcal{Z})
.

Combining both results, we recover the claim.

\square
